We now choose a gauge to fix some of the phases. This gauge applies to all four classes above. First, under gauge transformation
W(\bm{r}_{\mu})=1
for
\mu=0
,
W(\bm{r}_{i})=e^{i\sigma^{3}\psi_{i}r_{i}}
for
i=1,2,3
, where
\psi_{i}
is any constant phase, the values of
\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}
,
\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{2}}
and
\chi_{ST_{2}}
change. This means they are ineffective in labeling the PSG classes, and by properly choosing
\psi_{i}
we can set them to be

\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}=0,\ \ \ \chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{2}}=0,\ \ \ \chi_{ST_{2}}=0.

(100)

Then, we can use the IGG freedom (the freedom of choosing a global U(1) phase) to set

\rho_{0}=\theta_{2}=0\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \chi_{\overline{C}_{6}}=0.

(101)

Finally, using the “sublattice” gauge transformation

W(\bm{r}_{\mu})=e^{i\phi_{\mu}}

(102)

where
\phi_{\mu}
is any constant phase,
W_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})
for
\mu=1,2
and
W_{S}(\bm{r}_{\mu})
for
\mu=0
will transform as

\displaystyle(i\sigma^{1})^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}e^{i\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{1})\sigma^{3}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow e^{i\phi_{1}\sigma^{3}}(i\sigma_{1})^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}e^{i\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{2})\sigma^{3}}e^{-i\phi_{3}\sigma^{3}}

\displaystyle=(i\sigma^{1})^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}e^{i((-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}\phi_{1}+\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{1})-\phi_{3})\sigma^{3}},

(103a)

\displaystyle(i\sigma^{1})^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}e^{i\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{1})\sigma^{3}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow e^{i\chi_{1}\sigma^{3}}(i\sigma^{1})^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}e^{i\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{2})\sigma^{3}}e^{-i\phi_{1}\sigma^{3}}

\displaystyle=(i\sigma_{1})^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}e^{i((-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}\phi_{2}+\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{2})-\phi_{1})\sigma^{3}},

(103b)

\displaystyle(i\sigma_{1})^{w_{S}}e^{i\phi_{S}(\bm{r}_{0})\sigma^{3}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow e^{i\phi_{0}\sigma^{3}}(i\sigma^{1})^{w_{S}}e^{i\phi_{S}(\bm{r}_{0})\sigma^{3}}e^{-i\phi_{3}\sigma^{3}}

\displaystyle=(i\sigma^{1})^{w_{S}}e^{i((-1)^{w_{S}}\phi_{0}+\phi_{S}(\bm{r}_{0})-\phi_{3})\sigma^{3}}.

(103c)

Then, by properly choosing
\phi_{0,1,2,3}
we are able to set

\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}=\theta_{0}=0.

(104)

Eqs. (
100
), (
101
) and (
104
) significantly simply Eqs. (
92
)–(
99
), and furthermore allow them to be solved without any ambiguity. The final result is presented in Table
1
.

Appendix B
Solving U(1) PSG equations: adding time reversal

There is one complication when considering time reversal
\mathcal{T}
. Acting on spins, it is the anti-unitary operator

\mathcal{T}\colon\hat{\bm{S}}\rightarrow i\sigma^{y}\mathcal{K}\hat{\bm{S}}(-i\sigma^{y}\mathcal{K}),

(105)

where
\mathcal{K}
is the complex conjugation operator that complex conjugates everything on its right. This induces an action on
\Psi
as

\mathcal{T}\colon\Psi\rightarrow(i\sigma^{y})\mathcal{K}\Psi.

(106)

Introducing a gauge field associated to
\mathcal{T}
, we have

G_{\mathcal{T}}\circ\mathcal{T}\colon\Psi(\bm{r}_{\mu})\rightarrow(i\sigma^{y})\mathcal{K}\Psi(\bm{r}_{\mu})W_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu}).

(107)

Now we apply this to a mean-field bond:

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{T}}\circ\mathcal{T}\colon H^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}\rightarrow

(108)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\sigma^{\alpha})^{*}(i\sigma^{y})\mathcal{K}\Psi(\bm{r}_{\mu})W_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})u^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}W^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu})\Psi^{\dagger}(\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu})(-i\sigma^{y})\mathcal{K}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\sigma^{\alpha})^{*}\sigma^{y}\Psi(\bm{r}_{\mu})W^{*}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})\left(u^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)^{*}W^{T}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu})\Psi^{\dagger}(\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu})\sigma^{y}\right],

where we have noted that both the bond
u^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}
and
\sigma^{\alpha}
are complex-conjugated by
\mathcal{K}
. However we can get rid of this complex conjugation by defining a new gauge field
\widetilde{W}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})
by

W_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})\equiv i\sigma^{2}\widetilde{W}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu}).

(109)

Using the identity
\sigma^{2}u\sigma^{2}=u^{*}
for
u\in\mathrm{SU}(2)
, we have
W^{*}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=\widetilde{W}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})i\sigma^{2}
and

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}\left(u^{0}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)^{*}\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=-u^{0}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}},

(110)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}\left(u^{(i)}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right)^{*}\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=u^{(i)}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}},\quad i=x,y,z,

and

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(\sigma^{0})^{*}\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{0},

(111)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(\sigma^{i})^{*}\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=-\sigma^{i},\quad i=x,y,z,

we see that the form (
25
) is specially designed so that

\displaystyle

\displaystyle G_{\mathcal{T}}\circ\mathcal{T}\colon H^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\sigma^{\alpha}\Psi_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}u^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}\Psi^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right]

(112)

\displaystyle\rightarrow-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\sigma^{\alpha}\Psi_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}\widetilde{W}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})u^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}\widetilde{W}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}(\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu})\Psi^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}\right].

Therefore, with the redefined gauge
\widetilde{W}_{\mathcal{T}}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}=G_{\mathcal{T}}\circ\mathcal{T}
can be regarded as a unitary operation with an additional sign flip for the mean-field parameters (this sign flip keeps track of the anti-unitarity of
\mathcal{T}
).

For the rest of the appendices and in the main text, we will remove the “
\widetilde{\phantom{..}}
” in
\widetilde{W}_{\mathcal{T}}
and call it
W_{\mathcal{T}}
for simplicity. It is then easy to see that in a time reversal symmetric ansatz Eq. (
112
) leads to Eq. (
29
).

The SU(2) equations associated with Eqs. (
4j
) and (
4i
) are

\displaystyle{W}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})W_{T_{i}}(\bm{r}_{\mu}){W}_{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}[T_{i}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]W_{T_{i}}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=e^{i\sigma^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{i}}},

(113a)

\displaystyle{W}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})W_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{\mu}){W}_{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}[\overline{C}_{6}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]W_{\overline{C}_{6}}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=e^{i\sigma^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}},

(113b)

\displaystyle{W}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})W_{S}(\bm{r}_{\mu}){W}_{\mathcal{T}}^{-1}[S^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]W_{S}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=e^{i\sigma^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{T}S}},

(113c)

\displaystyle W^{2}_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=e^{i\sigma^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{T}}},

(113d)

where all the
\chi\in[0,2\pi)
for U(1) IGG, and
\chi\in\{0,\pi\}
for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
IGG.

The above analysis explains the general strategy of treating the projective time reversal operation. Below we specialize to case of a U(1) gauge group and solve the corresponding PSG equations (
113
). The general form of
W_{\mathcal{T}}
is

W_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=(i\sigma^{1})^{w_{\mathcal{T}}}e^{i\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})\sigma^{3}},

(114)

where
w_{\mathcal{T}}=0
or
1
. We now discuss these two cases separately.

When
w_{\mathcal{T}}=0
, Eq. (
113a
) gives

\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{\mu})+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{i}}r_{i}.

(115)

Then look at Eq. (
113d
). We can use the IGG freedom to set
\chi_{\mathcal{T}}=0
. This requires

2\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{\mu})=2\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{i}}=0,\quad\mu=0,1,2,3\text{ and }i=1,2,3.

(116)

Plug the form (
115
) in Eqs. (
113b
) and (
113c
), we get

\displaystyle(-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}+\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}+\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{2}}+\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}=0,

(117a)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{0})((-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}-1)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{3})-\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{1})-\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{2})-\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{3})

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}},

(117b)

\displaystyle-\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}((-1)^{w_{S}}+1)+(-1)^{w_{S}}\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{2}}((-1)^{w_{S}}+1)+(-1)^{w_{S}}\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}((-1)^{w_{S}}-1)=0,

(117c)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{3})(-1)^{w_{S}}-\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{0})-\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}(-1)^{w_{S}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{1})((-1)^{w_{S}}-1)-\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}(-1)^{w_{S}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{1})((-1)^{w_{S}}-1)-\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}(-1)^{w_{S}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{0})(-1)^{w_{S}}-\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{3})

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\mathcal{T}S},

(117d)

Eqs. (
117a
) and (
117c
) only give zero solution
\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}=\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{2}}=\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}=0
for any combinations of
w_{\overline{C}_{6}}=0,1
and
w_{S}=0,1
. Then, Eqs. (
117b
) and (
117d
) have the only solution
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{0})=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{1})=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{2})=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{3})
; and by further using the IGG freedom of time reversal we can set this phase to zero. Therefore the final solution for
w_{\mathcal{T}}=0
is
W_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=1
. However, this implies
u^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}=-u^{\alpha}_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}
according to Eq. (
29
), which gives vanishing mean-field ansätze. This indicates that
w_{\mathcal{T}}=0
is not physical.

Next consider the case
w_{\mathcal{T}}=1
. We again solve Eq. (
113a
) first:
consistency condition requires
-2\chi_{1}=0
, therefore
\chi_{1}=0
or
\pi
, and we have

\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{\mu})-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{i}}r_{i}.

(118)

Then we solve Eqs. (
113b
) and (
113c
).
In all four cases given by
w_{\overline{C}_{6}}=0
or
1
and
w_{S}=0
or
1
, we have
2\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}=2\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{2}}=2\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}=2\chi_{ST_{1}}=2\chi_{ST_{2}}=2\chi_{ST_{3}}=0
. Then, since
\chi_{1}=0
or
\pi
and
\chi_{ST_{1}}=0
or
2\pi/3
are the only possible values in the space group PSG solution, we see that

\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}=\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{2}}=\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}=\chi_{ST_{1}}=\chi_{ST_{2}}=\chi_{ST_{3}}=0.

(119)

Furthermore, analogous to the
w_{\mathcal{T}}=0
case, we obtain

\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}=\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{2}}=\chi_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}=0
and
\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{0})=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{1})=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{2})=\phi_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{0}_{3})
. Then we can always set this phase to zero using the IGG freedom of time reversal. This further implies

\chi_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}=\chi_{\mathcal{T}S}=0.

(120)

In conclusion, in the U(1) PSG case, adding time reversal symmetry does not introduce additional PSG parameters but further restricts
\chi_{1}=0
or
\pi
, and
\chi_{ST_{1}}=0
. The time reversal gauge part has the form (in our choice of gauge fixing)

W_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=i\sigma^{1}.

(121)

The final result is presented in Table
1
.

Appendix C
Solving
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
PSG equations: space group part

In solving the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
PSG equations (
78
), all the
\chi\in\{0,\pi\}
, therefore we introduce a short hand notation

\eta=e^{i\sigma^{3}\chi}=\pm 1\quad\text{for }\chi\text{'s in Eq.~\eqref{chi}}

(122)

The general form for
W_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})
and
W_{S}(\bm{r}_{\mu})
is given in Eqs. (
20b
) and (
20c
). Here, in order to clearly distinguish different notations, we will rewrite the SU(2) matrices at the origin
W_{\mathcal{O},\mu}
using a different symbol

g_{\mathcal{O},\mu}\equiv W_{\mathcal{O},\mu}\quad\text{for }\mathcal{O}=\overline{C}_{6},S.

(123)

The solution is in complete parallel to the U(1) PSG case which we briefly review below.

First solve Eq. (
78a
): using gauge freedom as in the U(1) case, we get

W_{T_{1}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=1,\quad W_{T_{2}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=\eta_{1}^{r_{1}},\quad W_{T_{3}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=\eta_{3}^{r_{1}}\eta_{2}^{r_{2}}.

(124)

Then solve Eq. (
78e
): plugging in the solution (
124
), we have

\displaystyle W_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})W^{-1}_{\overline{C}_{6}}[T_{2}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]\eta_{1}^{r_{1}}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}},

(125a)

\displaystyle W_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})\eta_{1}^{-(r_{2}+\delta_{\mu,2})}W^{-1}_{\overline{C}_{6}}[T_{3}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]\eta_{3}^{r_{1}}\eta_{2}^{r_{2}}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{2}},

(125b)

\displaystyle W_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})\eta_{3}^{-(r_{2}+\delta_{\mu,2})}\eta_{2}^{-(r_{3}+\delta_{\mu,3})}W^{-1}_{\overline{C}_{6}}[T_{1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}.

(125c)

Consistency condition requires
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=\eta_{3}
and we get

\displaystyle W_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})

\displaystyle=g_{\overline{C}_{6},\mu}\eta_{1}^{r_{1}(r_{2}+r_{3})}

(126)

\displaystyle\cdot(\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}\eta_{1}^{\delta_{\mu,2}+\delta_{\mu,3}})^{r_{1}}\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}^{r_{2}}(\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{2}}\eta_{1}^{\delta_{\mu,2}})^{r_{3}}.

Then solve Eq. (
78f
) and Eq. (
78g
): plugging in the solution (
124
), we have

\displaystyle W_{S}(\bm{r}_{\mu})W^{-1}_{S}[T_{1}T_{3}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]\eta_{1}^{-(r_{1}+r_{2}+1)}=\eta_{ST_{1}},

(127a)

\displaystyle W_{S}(\bm{r}_{\mu})\eta_{1}^{-(r_{1}+\delta_{\mu,1})}W^{-1}_{S}[T_{2}T_{3}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]\eta_{1}^{-(r_{1}+r_{2}+1)}\eta_{1}^{r_{1}}=\eta_{ST_{2}},

(127b)

\displaystyle W_{S}(\bm{r}_{\mu})\eta_{1}^{-(r_{1}+r_{2}+\delta_{\mu,1}+\delta_{\mu,2})}W^{-1}_{S}[T_{3}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]\eta_{1}^{-(r_{1}+r_{2})}=\eta_{ST_{3}}.

(127c)

The consistency condition is always satisfied, and we have

\displaystyle W_{S}(\bm{r}_{\mu})

\displaystyle=g_{S,\mu}\eta_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}(r_{1}+r_{2})(r_{1}+r_{2}+1)}(\eta_{ST_{1}}\eta_{ST_{3}}\eta_{1}^{\delta_{\mu,1}+\delta_{\mu,2}})^{r_{1}}

(128)

\displaystyle\cdot(\eta_{ST_{2}}\eta_{ST_{3}}\eta_{1}^{\delta_{\mu,2}})^{r_{2}}(\eta_{ST_{3}}\eta_{1}^{\delta_{\mu,1}+\delta_{\mu,2}})^{r_{3}}.

Now we are left with Eqs. (
78c
), (
78d
), (
78h
), and (
78i
). Plugging the solution for
W_{T_{1,2,3},\overline{C}_{6},S}
that we just obtained in these equations, we are led to the following constraints

\displaystyle\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{2}}\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}

\displaystyle=1,

(129a)

\displaystyle\eta_{ST_{3}}

\displaystyle=1,

(129b)

and the following equations to solve

\displaystyle g^{6}_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=(g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{\overline{C}_{6},2})^{2}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}},

(130a)

\displaystyle g^{2}_{S,1}\eta_{ST_{1}}\eta_{1}=g^{2}_{S,2}\eta_{ST_{2}}\eta_{1}=g_{S,3}g_{S,0}

\displaystyle=\eta_{S},

(130b)

\displaystyle g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}g_{S,0}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,2}g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}g_{S,1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g_{S,3}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S},

(130c)

\displaystyle\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}g^{3}_{\overline{C}_{6},0}g_{S,0}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g_{S,3}

\displaystyle=\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}},

(130d)

\displaystyle g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}g_{S,1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}g_{S,1}

\displaystyle=\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}},

(130e)

\displaystyle g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,2}g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,2}

\displaystyle=\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}},

(130f)

\displaystyle g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g_{S,3}g^{3}_{\overline{C}_{6},0}g_{S,0}\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}

\displaystyle=\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}.

(130g)

First let us use the IGG gauge freedom to simplify these equations. We can always set
\eta_{S}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{1}}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{2}}=1
, and by Eq. (
129a
) we also have
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}=1
. Then, under the “sublattice” gauge transformation (
102
), we can fix
g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}=1
,
g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}=1
, and
g_{S,0}=1
. Note this also implies
g_{S,3}=1
. To summarize, gauge fixing gives

g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}=g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}=g_{S,0}=g_{S,3}=1.

(131)

Now Eq. (
130
) is simplified to

\displaystyle g^{6}_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=g^{2}_{\overline{C}_{6},3}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}},

(132a)

\displaystyle g^{2}_{S,1}\eta_{ST_{1}}\eta_{1}=g^{2}_{S,2}\eta_{ST_{2}}\eta_{1}

\displaystyle=1,

(132b)

\displaystyle g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,2}g_{S,1}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S},

(132c)

\displaystyle g^{3}_{\overline{C}_{6},0}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}

\displaystyle=\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}},

(132d)

\displaystyle g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,1}

\displaystyle=\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}},

(132e)

\displaystyle g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,2}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,2}

\displaystyle=\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}.

(132f)

Next we claim that

\eta_{ST_{1}}=\eta_{ST_{2}}.

(133)

The proof proceeds as follows: If
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}}=1
then Eq. (
132a
) gives
g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}=\pm 1
, which together with Eqs. (
132e
) and (
132f
) proves (
133
) in Eq. (
132b
). If
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}}=-1
, then
g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}=i\bm{a}\cdot\bm{\sigma}
for some unit vector
\bm{a}
, and we proceed to prove
\eta_{ST_{1}}=\eta_{ST_{2}}
by contradiction: without loss of generality we assume
\eta_{ST_{1}}\eta_{1}=1=-\eta_{ST_{2}}\eta_{1}
, then
g_{S,1}\equiv\eta_{S,1}=\pm 1
and
g_{S,2}=i\bm{b}\cdot\bm{\sigma}
for some unit vector
\bm{b}
. Therefore Eq. (
132e
) gives
\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}=-1
, and Eq. (
132f
) gives
[(i\bm{a}\cdot\bm{\sigma})(i\bm{b}\cdot\bm{\sigma})]^{2}=-1
, which implies
\bm{a}\perp\bm{b}
and that
g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{S,2}=-i\bm{c}\cdot\bm{\sigma}
with
\bm{c}=\bm{b}\times\bm{a}
. Then from Eq. (
132c
) we get
g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=i\bm{c}\cdot\bm{\sigma}\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}\eta_{S,1}
, which contradicts Eq. (
132d
) given that
\bm{c}\perp\bm{a}
. Therefore Eq. (
133
) holds.

Next, depending on the value of
\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}
,
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}}
,
\eta_{ST_{1}}
and
\eta_{1}
, we have the following cases:

•

If
\eta_{1}\eta_{ST_{1}}=1
, then
g_{S,1}=\pm 1
and
g_{S,2}=\pm 1
, then we have

\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}=g^{2}_{\overline{C}_{6},3}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}},

(134)

meaning that Eq. (
132c
) to the cubic power gives
g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}^{3}=g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}^{-3}\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}g_{S,2}g_{S,1}
; together with Eq. (
134
) we see that

g_{S,1}g_{S,2}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}.

(135)

We have the following two cases after gauge fixing:

–

When
\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}}=1
,

(g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S_{2}})=(1,1,1,\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S});

(136)

–

When
\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}}=-1
,

(g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S_{2}})=(-i\sigma^{k},i\sigma^{k},1,\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}),

(137)

where
\sigma^{k}
can be any of the three Pauli matrices.

•

If
\eta_{1}\eta_{ST_{1}}=-1
, we have
g_{S,1}=i\bm{n}_{1}\cdot\bm{\sigma}
and
g_{S,2}=i\bm{n}_{2}\cdot\bm{\sigma}
for some unit vectors
\bm{n}_{1}
and
\bm{n}_{2}
. We have:

–

If
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}}=1
, then
g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}=\pm 1
therefore
\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}=-1
. Combine (
132c
) and (
132d
) we get
(g_{S,2}g_{S,1})^{-3}=-\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}
, which gives
g_{S,2}g_{S,1}=e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}j\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\sigma}}
for some unit vector
\bm{n}
, and
j=1,3
for
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}=1
while
j=0,2
for
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}=-1
. This then implies
\bm{n}_{2}\cdot\bm{n}_{1}+i(\bm{n}_{2}\times\bm{n}_{1})\cdot\bm{\sigma}=-e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}j\bm{n}\cdot\bm{\sigma}}
. After gauge fixing, we get

\displaystyle(g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S,2})

(138)

\displaystyle=(-\sigma^{k}(\cos\frac{\pi j}{3}\sigma^{k}+\sin\frac{\pi j}{3}\sigma^{k+1}),1,

\displaystyle i\sigma^{k},i(\cos\frac{\pi j}{3}\sigma^{k}+\sin\frac{\pi j}{3}\sigma^{k+1})),

where
\sigma^{k}
can be any of the three Pauli matrices. This gives eight classes depending on the values of
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}}
and
j
.

–

if
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}}=-1
, we have
g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}=i\bm{n}_{3}\cdot\bm{\sigma}
for some
\bm{n}_{3}
, and
g^{3}_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=-i\bm{n}_{3}\cdot\bm{\sigma}\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}
. Define
g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=e^{i\theta\bm{n}_{0}\cdot\bm{\sigma}}
, then we have
\cos 3\theta=0
,
\theta=\frac{\pi(2j+1)}{6}
, which gives
g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=e^{-i\frac{\pi(2j+1)}{6}(-1)^{j}\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}\bm{n}_{3}\cdot\bm{\sigma}}
, with independent
j=0,1,2
. Choose gauge fixing such that
g_{S,1}=i\sigma^{k}
where
\sigma^{k}
is any of the three Pauli matrices (we denote the corresponding
\bm{n}_{1}
as
\bm{x}_{k}
) and
g_{S,2}=e^{i\phi\sigma^{k-1}}i\sigma^{k}
for some
\phi
, then equation (
132e
) requires that
\bm{n}_{3}
be either parallel (anti-parallel) or perpendicular to
\bm{x}_{k}
, depending on the value of
\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}
. After gauge fixing, we obtain the following form:

*

If
\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}=1
, we have

(g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S,2})=(i\sigma^{k},i\sigma^{k},i\sigma^{k},\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}i\sigma^{k}),

(139)

the parameters
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}
and
\eta_{1}
give four independent classes.

*

If
\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}}=-1
, we have

\displaystyle(g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S,2})=

(140)

\displaystyle(e^{i\frac{\pi(2j+1)}{6}(-1)^{j}\sigma^{k-1}},i\sigma^{k-1},i\sigma^{k},

\displaystyle-\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}i\sigma^{k}e^{i\left(\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi(2j+1)}{6}(-1)^{j}\right)\sigma^{k-1}}),

where
j=0,1
together with
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}
and
\eta_{1}
gives eight independent classes.

The final result is presented in Table
2
.

Appendix D
Solving
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
PSG equations: adding time reversal

In this subsection we assume that all the space group PSG equations have been solved (and gauge-fixed). We then add time reversal to the PSG and solve all the PSG equations containing time reversal: just as in the U(1) case, we are solving Eqs. (
113
), but now the right-hand sides of these equations are
\pm 1
. From Eq. (
113a
) we get

W_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=g_{\mathcal{T},\mu}\eta_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}^{r_{1}}\eta_{\mathcal{T}T_{2}}^{r_{2}}\eta_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}^{r_{3}},

(141)

where we followed the notation in the last appendix:
g_{\mathcal{T},\mu}\equiv W_{\mathcal{T},\mu}
. Plug the form (
141
) in Eqs. (
113b
)–(
113d
), we obtain

\eta_{\mathcal{T}T_{1}}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}T_{2}}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}T_{3}}=0

(142)

and

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{T},0}g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}g^{-1}_{\mathcal{T},0}g^{-1}_{\overline{C}_{6},0}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathcal{T},1}g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}g^{-1}_{\mathcal{T},3}g^{-1}_{\overline{C}_{6},1}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathcal{T},2}g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}g^{-1}_{\mathcal{T},1}g^{-1}_{\overline{C}_{6},2}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathcal{T},3}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g^{-1}_{\mathcal{T},2}g^{-1}_{\overline{C}_{6},3}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}},

(143a)

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{T},0}g_{S,0}g^{-1}_{\mathcal{T},3}g^{-1}_{S,0}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathcal{T},1}g_{S,1}g^{-1}_{\mathcal{T},1}g^{-1}_{S,1}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathcal{T},2}g_{S,2}g^{-1}_{\mathcal{T},2}g^{-1}_{S,2}

\displaystyle=g_{\mathcal{T},3}g_{S,3}g^{-1}_{\mathcal{T},0}g^{-1}_{S,3}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S},

(143b)

\displaystyle g^{2}_{\mathcal{T},\mu}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathcal{T}}.

(143c)

Eq. (
142
) means that
W_{\mathcal{T}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=g_{\mathcal{T},\mu}
, which only depends on the sublattice indices. We now claim that
\eta_{\mathcal{T}}=-1
. Otherwise
\eta_{\mathcal{T}}=1
, then
g_{\mathcal{T},\mu}
has the form of a sign factor
\eta_{\mathcal{T},\mu}=\pm 1
times the identity matrix. Plugging this form in Eqs. (
143a
) and (
143b
) gives
1=\eta_{\mathcal{T},1}\eta_{\mathcal{T},3}=\eta_{\mathcal{T},2}\eta_{\mathcal{T},1}=\eta_{\mathcal{T},3}\eta_{\mathcal{T},2}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}
and
\eta_{\mathcal{T},0}\eta_{\mathcal{T},3}=1=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}
, meaning that all the sublattice signs
\eta_{\mathcal{T},\mu}
must be the same. As was argued in Appendix
B
this will lead to a vanishing mean-field ansätze. Therefore we must have
\eta_{\mathcal{T}}=-1
.

Recall that in classifying the space group PSG in Appendix
C
gauge fixing already gives
g_{\overline{C}_{6},1}=g_{\overline{C}_{6},2}=g_{S,0}=g_{S,3}=1
. Then Eqs. (
143a
) and (
143a
) enforce

g_{\mathcal{T},1}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}g_{\mathcal{T},0},\quad g_{\mathcal{T},2}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}g_{\mathcal{T},0},\quad g_{\mathcal{T},3}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}g_{\mathcal{T},0},

(144)

and the two equations reduce to

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{T},0}g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}g_{\mathcal{T},0},

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{T},0}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}g_{\mathcal{T},0},

(145)

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{T},0}g_{S,1}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}g_{S,1}g_{\mathcal{T},0},

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{T},0}g_{S,2}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}g_{S,2}g_{\mathcal{T},0}.

g_{\mathcal{T},0}
is then determined by
g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S,2}
and there are five cases listed below.

•

In the case
(\eta_{1}\eta_{ST_{1}},\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}},\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}})=(1,1,1)
, we have
(g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S,2})=(1,1,1,\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S})
, we see we must have
\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}=1
, and it is easy to use the remaining global SU(2) gauge freedom to set e.g.
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k}
, where
\sigma^{k}
can be any of the three Pauli matrices.

•

In the case
(\eta_{1}\eta_{ST_{1}},\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}},\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}})=(1,-1,-1)
, we have
(g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S,2})=(-i\sigma^{k},i\sigma^{k},1,\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S})
, it is easy to see from the expression of
g_{S,1}
and
g_{S,2}
that
\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}=1
. Then time reversal is either
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k}
which gives
\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}=1
, or
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k-1}
which gives
\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}=-1
. Note we have used the gauge freedom (rotating along
\sigma^{k}
axis) to set
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k-1}
.

•

In the case
(\eta_{1}\eta_{ST_{1}},\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}},\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}})=(-1,1,-1)
, we have
(g_{\overline{C}_{6},0},g_{\overline{C}_{6},3},g_{S,1},g_{S,2})=(-\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}e^{i\frac{\pi j}{3}\sigma^{k-1}},1,i\sigma^{k},i\sigma^{k}e^{i\frac{\pi j}{3}\sigma^{k-1}})
. When
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}=1
, we get
j=1,3
, and when
\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}=-1
, we get
j=0,2
. We always have
\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}=1
. If
j=1
or
2
, then we must have
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k-1}
, and
\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}=-1
; if
j=0
or
3
, then we can have
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k}
or
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k-1}
(after gauge fixing), which gives
g_{\mathcal{T}S}=1
or
g_{\mathcal{T}S}=-1
, respectively.

•

In the case
(\eta_{1}\eta_{ST_{1}},\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}},\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}})=(-,-,+)
,
g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=i\sigma^{k}
,
g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}=i\sigma^{k}
,
g_{S,1}=i\sigma^{k}
,
g_{S,2}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}i\sigma^{k}
. Two solutions exist: we can have either
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k}
corresponding to
\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}=1
, or
g_{\mathcal{T},0}=i\sigma^{k-1}
(after gauge fixing), corresponding to
\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}=\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}=-1
.

•

Lastly, in the case
(\eta_{1}\eta_{ST_{1}},\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}},\eta_{S\overline{C}_{6}})=(-1,-1,-1)
,
g_{\overline{C}_{6},0}=e^{i\frac{\pi(2j+1)}{6}(-1)^{j}\sigma^{k-1}}
,
g_{\overline{C}_{6},3}=i\sigma^{k-1}
,
g_{S,1}=i\sigma^{k}
,
g_{S,2}=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}i\sigma^{k+1}e^{i\frac{\pi(2j+1)}{6}(-1)^{j}\sigma^{k-1}}
.
When
j=0
we must have
\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}}=1
and
g=i\sigma^{k-1}
, which gives
\eta_{\mathcal{T}S}=-1
; when
j=1
three solutions exist: we can have
g=i\sigma^{k-1}
corresponding to
(\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}},\eta_{\mathcal{T}S})=(1,-1)
, or
g=i\sigma^{k}
corresponding to
(\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}},\eta_{\mathcal{T}S})=(-1,1)
, or
g=i\sigma^{k+1}
corresponding to
(\eta_{\mathcal{T}\overline{C}_{6}},\eta_{\mathcal{T}S})=(-1,-1)
.

The final result is presented in Table
2
.

Appendix E
0-flux symmetry properties

In this Appendix we study the symmetry transformation of the Hamiltonian and operators in the 0-flux ansätze in more detail. Under an arbitrary symmetry operation
\mathcal{O}
, following Eq. (
11
) we have

G_{\mathcal{O}}\circ\mathcal{O}\colon\Psi_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}\rightarrow U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{O}}\Psi_{\mathcal{O}(\bm{r}_{\mu})}W_{\mathcal{O},\mu}e^{i\sigma^{3}\phi_{\mathcal{O}}[\mathcal{O}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]},

(146)

In the case of U(1) PSG (with or without time reversal) and time reversal symmetric
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
PSG in our chosen gauge, we can always write, with the help of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
parameter
w_{\mathcal{O}}
,
W_{\mathcal{O},\mu}=(i\sigma^{1})^{w_{\mathcal{O}}}e^{i\sigma^{3}\phi^{0}_{\mathcal{O},\mu}}
, where
\phi^{0}_{\mathcal{O},\mu}
is some phase that can be absorbed into the definition of
\phi_{\mathcal{O}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})
, and we call
\bar{\phi}_{\mathcal{O}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})=\phi_{\mathcal{O}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})+\phi^{0}_{\mathcal{O},\mu}
. We have

G_{\mathcal{O}}\circ\mathcal{O}\colon\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}\end{array}\right)\rightarrow V_{\mathcal{O},w_{\mathcal{O}}}e^{i\tau^{3}\bar{\phi}_{\mathcal{O}}[\mathcal{O}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\mathcal{O}(\bm{r}_{\mu})}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{O}(\bm{r}_{\mu})}\end{array}\right),

(147)

with

V_{\mathcal{O},w_{\mathcal{O}}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{O}}&\\
&U^{T}_{\mathcal{O}}\end{array}\right)(-i\tau^{2}\sigma^{2})^{w_{\mathcal{O}}},

(148)

where we defined Pauli matrices
\tau^{1,2,3}
to act on the subspace of
f_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}
and
f^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}
. Note that
f^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}
is understood as
(f^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}})^{T}=\left(f^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}\uparrow},f^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}\downarrow}\right)^{T}
.

Then, Fourier transform gives

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\mu,\bm{k}}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\mu,-\bm{k}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\bm{r}}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{r}_{\mu}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}\end{array}\right)

(149)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{G_{\mathcal{O}}\circ\mathcal{O}}\frac{V_{\mathcal{O},w_{\mathcal{O}}}}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\bm{r}}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{r}_{\mu}}e^{i\tau^{3}\bar{\phi}_{\mathcal{O}}[\mathcal{O}(\bm{r}_{\mu})]}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\mathcal{O}(\bm{r}_{\mu})}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{O}(\bm{r}_{\mu})}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{V_{\mathcal{O},w_{\mathcal{O}}}}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\bm{r}}e^{-i[\bm{k}\cdot\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mathcal{O}(\mu)})-\tau^{3}\bar{\phi}_{\mathcal{O}}(\bm{r}_{\mathcal{O}(\mu)})]}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\bm{r}_{\mathcal{O}(\mu)}}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mathcal{O}(\mu)}}\end{array}\right),

Using the general structure

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}_{\overline{C}_{6}}(\bm{r}_{\mu})

\displaystyle=\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}r_{1}+\rho_{\mu},

(150)

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}_{S}(\bm{r}_{\mu})

\displaystyle=-\phi_{ST_{1}}r_{1}+3\phi_{ST_{1}}r_{2}+\theta_{\mu},

we have

\displaystyle\left[\bm{k}\cdot\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mathcal{O}(\mu)})-\tau^{3}\bar{\phi}_{\mathcal{O}}(\bm{r}_{\mathcal{O}(\mu)})\right]\Big|_{\mathcal{O}=\overline{C}_{6}}

(151)

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{C}_{6}(\bm{k})-\tau^{3}\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}\hat{\bm{b}}_{1}\right)\cdot\bm{r}-\bm{k}\cdot{\bm{e}}_{\mu}-\tau^{3}\rho_{\overline{C}_{6}},

and

\displaystyle\left[\bm{k}\cdot\mathcal{O}^{-1}(\bm{r}_{\mathcal{O}(\mu)})-\tau^{3}\bar{\phi}_{\mathcal{O}}(\bm{r}_{\mathcal{O}(\mu)})\right]\Big|_{\mathcal{O}=S}

(152)

\displaystyle=\left(S(\bm{k})-\tau^{3}(-\phi_{ST_{1}}\hat{\bm{b}}_{1}+3\phi_{ST_{1}}\hat{\bm{b}}_{2}\right)\cdot\bm{r}-\bm{k}\cdot{\bm{e}}_{\mu}-\tau^{3}\theta_{\overline{C}_{6}},

we have

\displaystyle G_{\overline{C}_{6}}

\displaystyle\circ\overline{C}_{6}\colon\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\mu,\bm{k}}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\mu,-\bm{k}}\end{array}\right)\rightarrow

(153)

\displaystyle V_{\overline{C}_{6},w_{\overline{C}_{6}}}e^{i(\bm{k}\cdot\bm{e}_{\mu}+\rho_{\overline{C}_{6}(\mu)})}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\overline{C}_{6}(\mu),\overline{C}_{6}(\bm{k})-\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}\hat{\bm{b}}_{1}}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\overline{C}_{6}(\mu),\overline{C}_{6}(-\bm{k})-\phi_{\overline{C}_{6}T_{3}}\hat{\bm{b}}_{1}}\end{array}\right),

and

\displaystyle G_{S}

\displaystyle\circ S\colon\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{\mu,\bm{k}}\\
f^{\dagger}_{\mu,-\bm{k}}\end{array}\right)\rightarrow

(154)

\displaystyle V_{S,w_{S}}e^{i(\bm{k}\cdot\bm{e}_{\mu}+\theta_{S(\mu)})}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{S(\mu),S(\bm{k})+\phi_{ST_{1}}(\hat{\bm{b}}_{1}-3\hat{\bm{b}}_{2})}\\
f^{\dagger}_{S(\mu),S(-\bm{k})+\phi_{ST_{1}}(\hat{\bm{b}}_{1}-3\hat{\bm{b}}_{2})}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle=(-A_{3h},-B_{3h},-D_{3h},-C_{3h},A_{3p}^{*},B_{3p}^{*},D_{3p}^{*},C_{3p}^{*})

\displaystyle=(-A_{3h},B_{3h},C_{3h},D_{3h},A_{3p}^{*},-B_{3p}^{*},-C_{3p}^{*},-D_{3p}^{*}),

\displaystyle(A’_{3h},B’_{3h},C’_{3h},D’_{3h},A’_{3p},B’_{3p},C’_{3p},D’_{3p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}(A’^{*}_{3h},-C’^{*}_{3h},-B’^{*}_{3h},-D’^{*}_{3h},A’^{*}_{3p},-C’^{*}_{3p},-B’^{*}_{3p},-D’^{*}_{3p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}(-A’_{3h},B’_{3h},C’_{3h},D’_{3h},A’^{*}_{3p},-B’^{*}_{3p},-C’^{*}_{3p},-D’^{*}_{3p}),

\displaystyle(A_{4h},B_{4h},C_{4h},D_{4h},A_{4p},B_{4p},C_{4p},D_{4p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}(A_{4h},B_{4h},C_{4h},-D_{4h},-A_{4p}^{*},-B_{4p}^{*},-C_{4p}^{*},D_{4p}^{*}),

\displaystyle(A_{5h},B_{5h},C_{5h},D_{5h},A_{5p},B_{5p},C_{5p},D_{5p})=\text{arbitrary},

\displaystyle(A_{6h},B_{6h},C_{6h},D_{6h},A_{6p},B_{6p},C_{6p},D_{6p})

\displaystyle=(-A_{6h},-C_{6h},-B_{6h},-D_{6h},A_{6p}^{*},C_{6p}^{*},B_{6p}^{*},D_{6p}^{*})

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}(-A_{6h},B_{6h},C_{6h},D_{6h},A_{6p}^{*},-B_{6p}^{*},-C_{6p}^{*},-D_{6p}^{*}),

\displaystyle(A_{7h},B_{7h},C_{7h},D_{7h},A_{7p},B_{7p},C_{7p},D_{7p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}(-A_{7h}^{*},B_{7h}^{*},-C_{7h}^{*},-D_{7h}^{*},A_{7p},-B_{7p},C_{7p},D_{7p})

\displaystyle=(-A_{7h}^{*},-B_{7h}^{*},D_{7h}^{*},C_{7h}^{*},A_{7p},B_{7p},-D_{7p},-C_{7p}),

\displaystyle(A’_{7h},B’_{7h},C’_{7h},D’_{7h},A’_{7p},B’_{7p},C’_{7p},D’_{7p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}(A’_{7h},-B’_{7h},-D’_{7h},-C’_{7h},A’_{7p},-B’_{7p},-D’_{7p},-C’_{7p}),

\displaystyle(A_{8h},B_{8h},C_{8h},D_{8h},A_{8p},B_{8p},C_{8p},D_{8p})

\displaystyle=(A_{8h}^{*},-D_{8h}^{*},C_{8h}^{*},-B_{8h}^{*},-A_{8p},D_{8p},-C_{8p},B_{8p});

(
163
)

•

(\chi_{ST_{1}},\chi_{S\overline{C}_{6}},\chi_{\overline{C}_{6}},j)=(\chi_{1}+\pi,\pi,\pi,0)
:

\displaystyle(\alpha_{h},\alpha_{p})=(\alpha_{h}^{*},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}\alpha_{p}^{*})=(\alpha_{h}^{*},-e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}\alpha_{p}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{h}^{*},\alpha_{p}^{*})=(\alpha_{h},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}\alpha_{p})=(\alpha_{h},-e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}\alpha_{p})

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{h},-\alpha_{p})=(\alpha_{h}^{*},-e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}\alpha_{p}^{*})=(\alpha_{h}^{*},e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}\alpha_{p}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{h}^{*},-\alpha_{p}^{*})=(\alpha_{h},-e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}\alpha_{p})=(\alpha_{h},e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}\alpha_{p}),

\displaystyle(a_{h},b_{h},c_{h},d_{h},a_{p},b_{p},c_{p},d_{p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}(-a_{h},b_{h},-c_{h},-d_{h},

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-e^{i\frac{4\pi}{3}}a_{p}^{*},e^{i\frac{4\pi}{3}}b_{p}^{*},-e^{i\frac{4\pi}{3}}c_{p}^{*},-e^{i\frac{4\pi}{3}}d_{p}^{*})

\displaystyle(A_{h},B_{h},C_{h},D_{h},A_{p},B_{p},C_{p},D_{p})

\displaystyle=(-A_{3h}^{*},B_{3h}^{*},C_{3h}^{*},D_{3h}^{*},-A_{3p},B_{3p},C_{3p},D_{3p}),

\displaystyle(A’_{3h},B’_{3h},C’_{3h},D’_{3h},A’_{3p},B’_{3p},C’_{3p},D’_{3p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}(A’^{*}_{3h},-C’^{*}_{3h},-B’^{*}_{3h},-D’^{*}_{3h},

\displaystyle\quad\qquad e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}A’^{*}_{3p},-e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}C’^{*}_{3p},-e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}B’^{*}_{3p},-e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}D’^{*}_{3p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}(-A’^{*}_{3h},B’^{*}_{3h},C’^{*}_{3h},D’^{*}_{3h},-A’_{3p},B’_{3p},C’_{3p},D’_{3p}),

\displaystyle(A_{4h},B_{4h},C_{4h},D_{4h},A_{4p},B_{4p},C_{4p},D_{4p})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}A_{4p}^{*},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}B_{4p}^{*},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}C_{4p}^{*},-e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}D_{4p}^{*}),

\displaystyle(A_{5h},B_{5h},C_{5h},D_{5h},A_{5p},B_{5p},C_{5p},D_{5p})=\text{arbitrary},

\displaystyle(A_{6h},B_{6h},C_{6h},D_{6h},A_{6p},B_{6p},C_{6p},D_{6p})

\displaystyle=(-A_{6h},-C_{6h},-B_{6h},-D_{6h},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}A_{6p}^{*},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}C_{6p}^{*},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}B_{6p}^{*},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}D_{6p}^{*})

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}(-A_{6h}^{*},B_{6h}^{*},C_{6h}^{*},D_{6h}^{*},-A_{6p},B_{6p},C_{6p},D_{6p}),

\displaystyle(A_{7h},B_{7h},C_{7h},D_{7h},A_{7p},B_{7p},C_{7p},D_{7p})

\displaystyle=\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}(-A_{7h},B_{7h},-C_{7h},-D_{7h},

\displaystyle\quad\qquad e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}A_{7p}^{*},-e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}B_{7p}^{*},e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}C_{7p}^{*},e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}D_{7p}^{*})

\displaystyle=(-e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}A_{7h}^{*},-e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}B_{7h}^{*},e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}D_{7h}^{*},e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}C_{7h}^{*},

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-A_{7p},-B_{7p},D_{7p},C_{7p}),

\displaystyle(A’_{7h},B’_{7h},C’_{7h},D’_{7h},A’_{7p},B’_{7p},C’_{7p},D’_{7p})

\displaystyle=e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}}\eta_{1}\eta_{\overline{C}_{6}S}(A’^{*}_{7h},-B’^{*}_{7h},-D’^{*}_{7h},-C’^{*}_{7h},-A’^{*}_{7p},B’^{*}_{7p},D’^{*}_{7p},C’^{*}_{7p}),

\displaystyle(A_{8h},B_{8h},C_{8h},D_{8h},A_{8p},B_{8p},C_{8p},D_{8p})

\displaystyle=(e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}A_{8h}^{*},-e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}D^{*}_{8h},e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}C^{*}_{8h},-e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}}B^{*}_{8h},

\displaystyle\quad\qquad-A_{8p},D_{8p},-C_{8p},B_{8p});

\displaystyle=(-A_{3h}^{*},B_{3h}^{*},C_{3h}^{*},D_{3h}^{*},-A_{3p},B_{3p},C_{3p},D_{3p}),

)

Appendix G
U(1) 0-flux MF ansätze with
m_{S}=1
has nodal star: a proof

Let us first state a Lemma: define a
6\times 6
matrix

A(x,y,z)\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}x(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3})&y(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})&z(\sigma_{3}-\sigma_{1})\\
z(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})&x(\sigma_{3}-\sigma_{1})&y(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3})\\
y(\sigma_{3}-\sigma_{1})&z(\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{3})&x(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})\end{array}\right),

(166)

(what we will use later is a special case with
(x,y,z)=(c,c^{\prime},c^{\prime*})
.)
then its inverse is

\left(A(x,y,z)\right)^{-1}=\frac{A(x^{2}-yz,z^{2}-xy,y^{2}-xz)}{2(x^{3}+y^{3}+z^{3}-3xyz)}.

(167)

Furthermore, define a
2\times 6
matrix

\displaystyle B(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\equiv

(168)

\displaystyle\left(\alpha\sigma^{0}+(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\cdot\bm{\sigma},\alpha\sigma^{0}+(\delta,\beta,\gamma)\cdot\bm{\sigma},\alpha\sigma^{0}+(\gamma,\delta,\beta)\cdot\bm{\sigma}\right)

and a
6\times 2
matrix

C(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha^{\prime}\sigma^{0}+(\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\cdot\bm{\sigma}\\
\alpha^{\prime}\sigma^{0}+(\delta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\cdot\bm{\sigma}\\
\alpha^{\prime}\sigma^{0}+(\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\cdot\bm{\sigma}\end{array}\right)

(169)

then for any complex
\zeta
we have (be wary of the switching
\delta\leftrightarrow\gamma
between
B
and
C
below)

B(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\left(A(x,y,z)\right)^{-1}C(\zeta\alpha,\zeta\beta,\zeta\delta,\zeta\gamma)=0.

(170)

The proof of this Lemma is elementary.

Now we use the lemma to prove that the existence of nodal star. The U(1) 0-flux mean-field ansätze correspond to an
8\times 8
matrix
\mathcal{H}_{\text{U(1)}}(\bm{k})
in the momentum space, with basis the parton operators
\left(f_{0\bm{k}\uparrow},f_{0\bm{k}\downarrow},f_{1\bm{k}\uparrow},f_{1\bm{k}\downarrow},f_{2\bm{k}\uparrow},f_{2\bm{k}\downarrow},f_{3\bm{k}\uparrow},f_{3\bm{k}\downarrow}\right)^{T}
. We abbreviate
\mathcal{H}_{\text{U(1)}}(\bm{k})
by
\mathcal{H}(\bm{k})
for the rest of this appendix. For U(1) 0-flux states with the PSG number
w_{S}=1
, we have the three-fold rotation symmetry
C_{3}
along
(1,1,1)
axis, and the screw symmetry
S
:

\displaystyle W^{\dagger}_{C_{3}}(\bm{k})\mathcal{H}(\bm{k})W_{C_{3}}(\bm{k})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}(k_{z},k_{x},k_{y}),

(171)

\displaystyle W^{\dagger}_{S}(\bm{k})\mathcal{H}(\bm{k})W_{S}(\bm{k})

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{H}^{T}(-k_{y},-k_{x},k_{z}),

notice the second line is
specific
to
w_{S}=1
. Now define the operation
R\equiv S\circ C_{3}\circ C_{3}\circ S\circ C_{3}\circ S
. Then we notice that
R
and
C_{3}
both map the momentum
(k,k,k)
back to itself:

\displaystyle W^{\dagger}_{C_{3}}(k,k,k)\mathcal{H}(k,k,k)W_{C_{3}}(k,k,k)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}(k,k,k),

(172)

\displaystyle W^{\dagger}_{R}(k,k,k)\mathcal{H}(k,k,k)W_{R}(k,k,k)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{H}^{T}(k,k,k).

Now, assume the most general form of an
8\times 8
hermitian matrix
\mathcal{H}_{\text{U(1)}}(k,k,k)=\left[h_{\mu\nu}\right]
, where
h_{\mu\nu}
are
2\times 2
blocks with
\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3
. Using the special form of
W_{C_{3}}(k,k,k)
and
W_{R}(k,k,k)
, we can show step by step the following:

•

h_{00}=0
,
h_{11}=c(\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{3})
,
h_{22}=c(\sigma^{3}-\sigma^{1})
,
h_{33}=c(\sigma^{1}-\sigma^{2})
, where
c
is some real parameter;

•

h_{12}=c^{\prime}(\sigma^{1}-\sigma^{2})
,
h_{13}=c^{\prime*}(\sigma^{3}-\sigma^{1})
,
h_{23}=c^{\prime}(\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{3})
, where
c^{\prime}
is some complex parameter;

•

h_{01}=a^{\prime\prime}\sigma^{0}+(b^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime\prime})\cdot\bm{\sigma}
,
h_{02}=a^{\prime\prime}\sigma^{0}+(d^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime})\cdot\bm{\sigma}
, and
h_{03}=a^{\prime\prime}\sigma^{0}+(c^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\cdot\bm{\sigma}
, where
(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime\prime})
are complex parameters satisfying
a^{\prime\prime}e^{ik}=-a^{\prime\prime*}
,
e^{ik}(-b^{\prime\prime},-d^{\prime\prime},-c^{\prime\prime})=(b^{\prime\prime*},c^{\prime\prime*},d^{\prime\prime*})
.

Therefore the Hamiltonian can be written in the form

\mathcal{H}(k,k,k)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&B\\
C&A\end{array}\right),

(173)

where
A
is a
6\times 6
block containing
h_{ij}
blocks with
i,j=1,2,3
,
B
is a
2\times 6
block containing
h_{01},h_{02}
and
h_{03}
, and
C=B^{\dagger}
. Using the above Lemma, we can show that
BA^{-1}C=0_{2\times 2}
, therefore using the standard matrix decomposition, we have

\mathcal{H}(k,k,k)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1_{2\times 2}&BA^{-1}\\
0&1_{6\times 6}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
0&A\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1_{2\times 2}&0\\
A^{-1}C&1_{6\times 6}\end{array}\right),

(174)

we see that the rank of
\mathcal{H}_{\text{U(1)}}(k,k,k)
is smaller or equal to 6, i.e.
\mathcal{H}_{\text{U(1)}}(k,k,k)
at least has two zero eigenvalues. The existence of zero eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}_{\text{U(1)}}(-k,-k,k)
,
\mathcal{H}_{\text{U(1)}}(-k,k,-k)
and
\mathcal{H}_{\text{U(1)}}(k,-k,-k)
then follows.

Appendix H
Gauge invariance at one-loop level

It is know that for a generic Hamiltonian couple to a U(1) gauge field, gauge invariance requires that 1) the photon self energy
\Pi(q)
vanishes when the photon external momentum
\bm{q}
vanishes and that 2) Ward identity holds. These statements holds perturbatively at each loop level. Here we explicitly proof these two statements for non-interacting fermions coupled to a U(1) gauge field at one-loop level. For a generic tight binding Hamiltonian

H_{0}=\sum_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}c^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}h_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}c_{\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}},

(175)

the U(1) gauge coupling is introduced via the Peierl’s substitution:

H[A]=\sum_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}c^{\dagger}_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}h_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}e^{iA_{\bm{r}_{\mu},\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}}c_{\bm{r}^{\prime}_{\nu}}+\sum_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}A_{0,\bm{r}_{\mu}}n_{\bm{r}_{\mu}}

(176)

the spatial fluctuation of the gauge field is small at short distance, suggesting that we can expand the exponential for the gauge field. To quadratic order of
A
we obtain

H[A]=H_{0}+\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{q}}A^{i}(-q)c^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}\frac{\partial h(\bm{k})}{\partial k_{i}}c_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}+\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{q}}iA^{0}(-q)c^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}c_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}+\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{q}}A^{i}(q)A^{j}(q^{\prime})c^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}^{\prime}}\frac{\partial^{2}h(\bm{k})}{\partial k_{j}\partial k_{i}}c_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}}+O(A^{3}),

(177)

up to this order we have the usual minimal coupling vertex
Ac^{\dagger}c
as well as a
diamagnetic
vertex
A^{2}c^{\dagger}c
. These two vertices lead to the two diagrams at one-loop shown in Fig.
3
: the usual vacuum polarization bubble (left) and the “tadpole” diagram (right). Note that the diamagnetic term vanishes for a Dirac Hamiltonian since it is linear in momentum. In the following we show that the contribution of these two one-loop diagrams cancel each other at
q=0
, and furthermore the sum of the them at finite momentum and frequency satisfies the Ward identity.

The vacuum polarization bubble diagram in Fig.
3
originates from the
Ac^{\dagger}c
term. The vertex expression
\gamma_{\mu}(\bm{k})=\delta_{\mu 0}+\delta_{\mu,i}\partial_{k_{i}}h
, i.e. the vertex is unity for the temporal component
\mu=0
and is
\partial_{k_{i}}h
for the spatial component. The “tadpole” diagram originates from the
A^{2}c^{\dagger}c
term. The vertex expression is
\gamma_{\mu\nu}(\bm{k})=\delta_{\mu,i}\delta_{\nu,j}\partial_{k_{i}}\partial_{k_{j}}h
, i.e. the vertex only exists for
\mu,\nu
both being spatial indices, with vertex expression
\partial_{k_{i}}\partial_{k_{j}}h
. The two diagrams have the following expression

\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu\nu}(q)=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}[\gamma_{\mu}(\bm{k})G_{0}(k+q/2)\gamma_{\nu}(\bm{k})G_{0}(k-q/2)],\quad\Pi^{(1)}_{2,\mu\nu}(q)=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}[\gamma_{\mu\nu}(\bm{k})G_{0}(k-q)].

(178)

We now show that
\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu\nu}(q=0)+\Pi^{(1)}_{2,\mu\nu}(q=0)=0
.
First of all, when one of the
\mu,\nu
is a temporal component, say
\nu=0
, then
\Pi^{(1)}_{2,\mu\nu}=0
and we are only left with
\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu 0}(\bm{q}=0)
: in the following we write
k_{0}=\omega
. We have

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu 0}(q=0)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}[\gamma_{\mu}(\bm{k})G^{2}_{0}(\bm{k})]=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\gamma_{\mu}(\bm{k})\left(\frac{1}{\omega-h(\bm{k})}\right)^{2}\right]=-\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\gamma_{\mu}(\bm{k})\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-h(\bm{k})}\right)\right]

(179)

\displaystyle=-\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left\{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\gamma_{\mu}(\bm{k})\frac{1}{\omega-h(\bm{k})}\right]\right\}=-\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\gamma_{\mu}(\bm{k})\frac{1}{\omega-h(\bm{k})}\right]\Bigg|^{\omega=+\infty}_{\omega=-\infty}=0,

where we have used the fact that if
h
is diagonalized as
h=U^{\dagger}\Lambda U
, then
\frac{1}{(\omega-h)^{2}}=U^{\dagger}\frac{1}{(\omega-\Lambda)^{2}}U=U^{\dagger}\left(-\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-\Lambda}\right)\right)U=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-h}\right)
. We therefore see that
\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu 0}(\bm{q}=0)=0
for any
\mu
. This means that
\Pi^{(1)}_{1,0\nu}(q=0)=0
for any
\nu
. Then, we look at spatial components (note we have suppressed the arguments
\bm{k}
below):

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}_{1,ij}(q=0)+\Pi^{(1)}_{2,ij}(q=0)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\partial_{k_{i}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\partial_{k_{j}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\right]+\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\partial_{k_{i}}\partial_{k_{j}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\right]

(180)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\partial_{k_{i}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\partial_{k_{j}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\right]-\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\partial_{k_{j}}h\partial_{k_{i}}\left(\frac{1}{\omega-h}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\partial_{k_{i}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\partial_{k_{j}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\right]-\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\partial_{k_{j}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\partial_{k_{i}}h\frac{1}{\omega-h}\right]

\displaystyle=0,

where we have used the fact that
\partial(K^{-1})=-K^{-1}(\partial K)K^{-1}
for any (non-singular) matrix
K
. Therefore we have proved that the photon self-energy vanishes at one-loop level when photon external momentum is zero.

Next we show that Ward identity holds at one-loop level
q_{\mu}\left(\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu i}({q})-\Pi^{(1)}_{2,\mu i}({q})\right)=0
. First, only the vacuum polarization diagram contributes to the
\mu 0
component:

q_{\mu}\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu 0}({q})=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{-q_{0}+q_{i}\partial_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k})}{(k_{0}+q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2))(k_{0}-q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2))}\right],

(181)

note that

(k_{0}+q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2))-(k_{0}-q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2))=q_{0}-q_{i}\partial_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k})+o(q),

(182)

this means that

q_{\mu}\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu 0}({q})=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{1}{k_{0}+q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2)}-\frac{1}{k_{0}-q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)}\right],

(183)

which gives zero since the two terms only differ by a shift. Similarly, we have

q_{\mu}\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu i}=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(-q_{0}+q_{j}\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k})\right)\frac{1}{k_{0}+q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2)}\partial_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k})\frac{1}{k_{0}-q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)}\right],

(184)

and similar to the
\mu 0
component case, we get

q_{\mu}\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu i}=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\partial_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k})\left(\frac{1}{k_{0}+q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2)}-\frac{1}{k_{0}-q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)}\right)\right],

(185)

on the other hand we have
\frac{1}{2}q_{i}\partial_{k_{i}}\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k})=\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2)-\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k})+o(q)=\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k})-\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)+o(q)
, and

\displaystyle q_{j}\Pi^{(1)}_{2,ji}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{q_{j}\partial_{k_{j}}\partial_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k})}{k_{0}-q_{0}-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q})}\right]=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)-\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}-\bm{q})+\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}-\bm{q})-\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}-3\bm{q}/2)}{k_{0}-q_{0}-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q})}\right]

(186)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k})-\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)}{k_{0}-q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)}\right]+\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2)-\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k})}{k_{0}+q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2)}\right],

where we have shifted the integral variables. Therefore we see that (denote
\Pi^{(1)}_{2,0\mu}=0
)

q_{\mu}\left(\Pi^{(1)}_{1,\mu i}({q})-\Pi^{(1)}_{2,\mu i}({q})\right)=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{\partial_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2)}{k_{0}+q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2)}-\frac{\partial_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)}{k_{0}-q_{0}/2-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2)}\right],

(187)

which gives zero on the Brillouin zone. Therefore Ward identity holds at one-loop level.

Appendix I
Deriving the photon vacuum bubble: scaling analysis

We study the
00
component of the vacuum polarization diagram: after completing the frequency integral, we have (again in imaginary time)

D(q)\equiv\Pi^{(1)}_{1,00}(q)=-\pi\mathrm{Re}\left[\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1-\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}\cdot\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+iq_{0}}\right],

(188)

with
\bm{\hat{d}}=\bm{d}/|\bm{d}|
. To separate the contribution along the nodal line and that in the vicinity of the
\Gamma
point, we use the identity
1=\frac{x}{a+x}+\frac{a}{a+x}
, where we set
a=c\bm{q}^{2}
and
x=|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+iq_{0}
, so that

D(q)=D_{1}(q)+D_{2}(q),

(189)

with

\displaystyle D_{1}(q)

\displaystyle=-\pi\mathrm{Re}\left[\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1-\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}\cdot\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+iq_{0}+c\bm{q}^{2}}\right],

(190a)

\displaystyle D_{2}(q)

\displaystyle=-\pi\mathrm{Re}\left[\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{c\bm{q}^{2}(1-\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}\cdot\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2})}{(|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+iq_{0})(|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+iq_{0}+c\bm{q}^{2})}\right],

(190b)

the choice of
a=c\bm{q}^{2}
is made to agree with the scaling
\bm{d}_{\bm{k}}\propto\bm{q}^{2}
near the
\Gamma
point (see Eq. (
46
)) and guarantees that
D_{2}
extracts the contribution in vicinity of the
\Gamma
point.
Having in mind that at small
\bm{q}
the leading order
\bm{q}
result is isotropic in
\bm{q}
. Therefore we choose a specific direction for
\bm{q}
:
\bm{q}=q_{z}\bm{\hat{z}}
.

We first look at
D_{2}(q)
: since
D_{2}(q)
is supported in vicinity of the
\Gamma
point, we expand
\bm{d}_{\bm{k}}
as in Eq. (
46
). Rescaling
\bm{k}=\bm{x}q_{z}
and
\bm{d}_{\bm{k}}=q_{z}^{2}\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{k}}
, we have

D_{2}(q)\sim-c\pi|q_{z}|\mathrm{Re}\left[\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{x}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1-\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\bm{x}+\bm{\hat{z}}/2}\cdot\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\bm{x}-\bm{\hat{z}}/2}}{(|\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{x}+\bm{\hat{z}}/2}|+|\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{x}-\bm{\hat{z}}/2}|+iq_{0}/\bm{q}^{2})(|\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{x}+\bm{\hat{z}}/2}|+|\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{x}-\bm{\hat{z}}/2}|+iq_{0}/|\bm{q}|+c)}\right]+O(q^{2}),

(191)

First look at the case of
q_{0}=0
. In this case, the integral in
D_{2}(q)
is well behaved, which can be easily seen in the original expression (
190b
): the only singularity comes from
|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|=0
, or
|\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}||\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|=0
, which gives isolated points
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(\pm 1/2,\pm 1/2,0)
. Expand around these points:
x_{1}=\pm 1/2+\eta\xi_{1}
,
x_{2}=\pm 1/2+\eta\xi_{2}
, and
x_{3}=\eta\xi_{3}
, we see that
|\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}||\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|\sim\eta f(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
where the function
f
is well behaved; therefore these singularities are integrable. We can also relax the integral for
\bm{x}
to the infinite plane and still get finite results. Therefore the integral in
D_{2}(q)
is well behaved, and
D_{2}(\bm{q},q_{0}=0)
scales linearly with
|\bm{q}|
.

Next, we need to extract the scaling behavior at finite frequency; analytic continuation
iq_{0}\rightarrow\omega+i\delta
is needed. First, the real part of
D_{2}(q)
is of the form
D_{2}(q)=|\bm{q}|f(\omega/\bm{q}^{2})
, where
f
is a well-behaved function whose value is always finite (according to the
q_{0}=0
analysis above). However it might be useful to see how the actual scaling looks like. What we care is when
w=\omega/\bm{q}^{2}\ll 1
since this is the regime that
D_{2}(q)
has both real and imaginary parts. In this regime,
1-\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\bm{x}+\bm{\hat{z}}/2}\cdot\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\bm{x}-\bm{\hat{z}}/2}
is finite (numerically verified), therefore the scaling is determined simply by
\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{x}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{(|\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{x}+\bm{\hat{z}}/2}|+|\bm{\epsilon}_{\bm{x}-\bm{\hat{z}}/2}|\pm(w+i\delta))}
. The result is
\sim\int\frac{r^{2}dr}{r\pm(w+i\delta)}\sim(\mp wr+r^{2}/2+w^{2}\ln(\pm w+r))|^{R}_{0}+i\pi w^{2}\mathrm{sgn}(w)
, we see that we have scaling
w^{2}\ln|w|+i\pi w^{2}\text{sgn}(w)
. Note that the real part
w^{2}\ln|w|
is in addition to other contributions in
f(w)
. Therefore in the limit
\omega\rightarrow 0
, we recover the scaling
D_{2}(q)\sim|q_{z}|
.

Next we deal with
D_{1}(q)
. The numerator can be simplified: notice that
\bm{d}^{2}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}+\bm{d}^{2}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}-2|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}||\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|=(|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|-|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|)^{2}\sim(\sin q\sin k)^{2}
, and
(\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}-\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2})^{2}=8\sin^{2}k_{z}\sin^{2}\frac{q_{z}}{2}
, therefore we can substitute

1-\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}\cdot\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}=\frac{-(|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|-|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|)^{2}+(\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}-\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2})^{2}}{2|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}||\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|}\sim\frac{\sin^{2}q\sin^{2}k}{2|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}||\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|}.

(192)

since
D_{1}(q)
receives contribution mainly along the line, we can make the rescaling
k_{1}=k_{3}+qx_{1}
,
k_{2}=k_{3}+qx_{2}
and the approximation
|d_{\bm{k}\pm\bm{q}/2}|=|\bm{q}||\sin k_{3}|f_{\pm}(x_{1},x_{2})+O(q^{3})
, where
f_{\pm}(x_{1},x_{2})=\sqrt{(1\mp 2(x_{1}+x_{2})+4x_{1}^{2}+4x_{2}^{2}-4x_{1}x_{2})/2}
, we further have

1-\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}\cdot\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}\sim\frac{1}{f_{+}(\bm{x})f_{-}(\bm{x})}.

(193)

Then after analytic continuation to real frequency we have

D_{1}(q\bm{\hat{z}},\omega)=-\frac{|\bm{q}|}{2\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}\bm{x}\frac{1}{f_{+}(\bm{x})f_{-}(\bm{x})}\int^{\pi}_{0}dk_{3}\left(\frac{1}{|\sin k_{3}|(f_{+}+f_{-})+\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}+c|\bm{q}|+i\delta}+\frac{1}{|\sin k_{3}|(f_{+}+f_{-})-\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}+c|\bm{q}|-i\delta}\right)

(194)

(following Leon) the first integral gives (here we only consider
\omega\ll q\ll 1
)

D_{1}^{(1)}=-\frac{|\bm{q}|}{\pi^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{1}{|\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}+c|\bm{q}||}\right)C_{0}-i\frac{|\bm{q}|}{2\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}\bm{x}\frac{\Theta(0<-\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}-c|\bm{q}|<f_{+}+f_{-})}{f_{+}(\bm{x})f_{-}(\bm{x})\sqrt{(f_{+}(\bm{x})+f_{-}(\bm{x}))^{2}-(\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}+c|\bm{q}|)^{2}}},

(195)

where we defined

C_{0}=\int d^{2}\bm{x}\frac{1}{f_{+}(\bm{x})f_{-}(\bm{x})(f_{+}(\bm{x})+f_{-}(\bm{x}))}.

(196)

the imaginary part vanishes for
|\omega|\ll\bm{q}^{2}
since in this regime the Heaviside function has zero support. When
|\bm{q}|\gg\omega\gg\bm{q}^{2}
, we can ignore the
c|\bm{q}|^{2}
term, and we have

D_{1}(q\bm{\hat{z}},\omega)=-|\bm{q}|\ln\left(\frac{1}{|c^{2}\bm{q}^{2}-\frac{\omega^{2}}{q^{2}}|}\right)C_{0}-i|\bm{q}|\text{sgn}(\omega)\left[g\left(\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}\right)\Theta(|\bm{q}|\gg|\omega|>c\bm{q}^{2})+\frac{\omega^{2}}{\bm{q}^{4}}\Theta(|\omega|\ll\bm{q}^{2})\right],

(197)

where
g(x)
is a function of
x=\omega/g
. We verify numerically that
g(\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|})=C_{0}+\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|})^{2}
. As we will show in the next appendix, a pure nodal line approximation can recover the calculation here by introducing a cutoff
\theta_{0}
for integrals along the nodal line at the
\Gamma
point of the form
\theta_{0}\sim\bm{q}
.

To summarize, the
00
component
D(q)=\Pi^{00}(q)
receives contribution of
D_{2}(q)\sim|\bm{q}|f(\omega/q^{2})
near the
\Gamma
point and receives contribution
D_{1}=-|\bm{q}|\ln\left(\frac{1}{|c^{2}|\bm{q}|^{2}-\omega^{2}/q^{2}|}\right)-i|\bm{q}|g(\omega/q)\Theta(|\bm{q}|\gg|\omega|>c\bm{q}^{2})
along the nodal lines. At small frequency
\omega\ll q^{2}
,
D(q)
is real and is dominated by the nodal line (
q\ln(1/q)
vs
q
).

The
0i
and
ij
components can be analyzed in the same way. We have

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}_{1,0i}(q)

\displaystyle=-i\pi\mathrm{Re}\left[\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\frac{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|-|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|}\frac{{C}_{i}(\bm{k},\bm{q})}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}||\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|}-\frac{D_{i}(\bm{k},\bm{q})}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}||\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|}}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+iq_{0}}\right]q_{0}\sin k_{i},

(198a)

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}_{1,ii}(q)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\mathrm{Re}\left[\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1-\hat{B}_{i}(\bm{k},\bm{q})}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+iq_{0}}\right]\sin^{2}k_{i},

(198b)

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}_{1,ij}(q)

\displaystyle=\pi\mathrm{Re}\left[\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1-2\hat{B}_{i}(\bm{k},\bm{q})-2\hat{B}_{j}(\bm{k},\bm{q})}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}|+|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|+iq_{0}}\right]\sin k_{i}\sin k_{j},

(198c)

where (it is understood
i+3\equiv i
)

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{i}

\displaystyle=\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}\cdot\bm{\hat{d}}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}-3\hat{d}^{i}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}\hat{d}^{i}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2},

(199a)

\displaystyle{C}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[({d}^{i+2}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}+{d}^{i+2}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2})-({d}^{i+1}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}+{d}^{i+1}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2})],

(199b)

\displaystyle{D}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[({d}^{i+2}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}-{d}^{i+2}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2})-({d}^{i+1}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}-{d}^{i+1}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2})],

(199c)

Due to the appearance of
\sin k_{i}
coming from the vertex expressions, the
\Gamma
point will contribute at a much higher order:
\Pi^{(1)}_{1,0i}\sim q^{2}
, and
\Pi^{(1)}_{1,ij}\sim q^{3}
. Furthermore, the nodal line contribution becomes

\Pi^{(1)}_{1,0i}=-iq_{0}\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}\bm{x}\frac{c_{0i}(\bm{x})}{f_{+}(\bm{x})f_{-}(\bm{x})}\int^{\pi}_{0}dk_{3}\left(\frac{1}{|\sin k_{3}|(f_{+}+f_{-})+\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}+c|\bm{q}|+i\delta}+\frac{1}{|\sin k_{3}|(f_{+}+f_{-})-\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}+c|\bm{q}|-i\delta}\right)

(200)

Note that the numerator of Eq. (
198a
) gives

\sim\frac{\sin q\sin k}{|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}||\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|}

(201)

(c.f. Eq. (
192
)), therefore it requires an extra
|\bm{q}|
and an extra
\sin k_{i}
to cancel the
|\bm{q}|^{2}\sin^{2}k_{3}
coming from
|\bm{d}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}||\bm{d}_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}|
in Eq. (
201
). Then, for the
ij
components

\Pi^{(1)}_{1,ij}=-\frac{|\bm{q}|}{2\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}\bm{x}\frac{c_{ij}(\bm{x})}{f_{+}(\bm{x})f_{-}(\bm{x})}\int^{\pi}_{0}dk_{3}\left(\frac{\sin^{2}k_{3}}{|\sin k_{3}|(f_{+}+f_{-})+\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}+c|\bm{q}|+i\delta}+\frac{\sin^{2}k_{3}}{|\sin k_{3}|(f_{+}+f_{-})-\frac{\omega}{|\bm{q}|}+c|\bm{q}|-i\delta}\right)

(202)

which gives

\Pi^{(1)}_{1,0i}\sim\omega n_{0i}(\bm{\hat{q}})\ln(1/|w|^{2})+i\omega g(w),\qquad\Pi^{(1)}_{1,ij}\sim|\bm{q}|\left(n_{ij}(\bm{\hat{q}})(2-\pi w)+m_{ij}(\bm{\hat{q}})w^{2}\ln(1/w)\right)-i|\bm{q}|w^{2}g(w),

(203)

where
w=\omega/|\bm{q}|\ll 1
and
|\bm{q}|\gg|\omega|\gg c\bm{q}^{2}
(The discrete poles when
|\omega|\ll\bm{q}^{2}
are not included here). We see that the nodal line contribution also dominates in these components. Note the above expressions hold only in the scaling sense; for example, the
|\bm{q}|
factor in
\Pi^{(1)}_{1,ij}
must have a component dependent form in order for Ward identity to hold. On the other hand, these expressions already have the right scaling for Ward identity to hold. The detailed form of these scaling functions can be found in the next section.

Therefore in photon thermodynamics we just have to concentrate on the nodal line and not the
\Gamma
region. This validates the QED calculation in the next section.

Finally, we mention that the momentum dependent part of
\Pi_{2,\penalty\hskip 0.0pti\kern-0.16391pt\penalty\hskip 0.0ptj}
starts to contribute at quadratic order in
q^{2}
: this is because due to the special form of
h(\bm{k})
we have

\Pi^{(1)}_{2,ij}=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{\partial_{k_{i}}\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k})}{k_{0}-q_{0}-h(\bm{k}-\bm{q})}\right]=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{\partial_{k_{i}}\partial_{k_{j}}h(\bm{k}+\bm{q}))}{k_{0}-h(\bm{k})}\right]=\delta_{ij}\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k}+\bm{q})}{k_{0}-h(\bm{k})}\right],

(204)

since
h(-\bm{k})=h(\bm{k})
, if we expand
\partial^{2}_{k_{i}}h(\bm{k}+\bm{q})
, the term linear in
\bm{q}
is odd in
\bm{k}
vanishes after the integral over
\bm{k}
, leaving the leading order contribution quadratic in
\bm{q}
.

Appendix J
Deriving the photon vacuum bubble: nodal line approximation

We concluded in the last Appendix that the calculation of photon self-energy at one-loop level amounts to calculating the momentum dependent part of the vacuum polarization bubble. This is the diagram resulted from the minimal coupling term
A_{i}(-\bm{q})\partial_{k_{i}}\mathcal{H}(\bm{k})\psi^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2}\psi_{\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2}
. The expressions for this diagram and the vertex have been given in Eqs. (
52
) and (
53
). The bare Green’s function for the spinons has the explicit form

G_{0}(k)=\frac{1}{ik_{0}-\mathcal{H}}=\frac{ik_{0}+\mathcal{H}}{-k_{0}^{2}-E^{2}}=-\frac{ik_{0}+\cos k_{1}(\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{3})+\cos k_{2}(\sigma^{3}-\sigma^{1})+\cos k_{3}(\sigma^{1}-\sigma^{2})}{k_{0}^{2}+(\cos k_{1}-\cos k_{2})^{2}+(\cos k_{2}-\cos k_{3})^{2}+(\cos k_{3}-\cos k_{1})^{2}}.

(205)

To evaluate the vacuum polarization bubble near the nodal star region, we first need to write the momentum in local coordinates. Denote the nodal line by
(\varsigma_{1},\varsigma_{2},\varsigma_{3})
, where
\varsigma_{1,2,3}=\pm 1
labels different nodal lines. For each nodal line, we denote

\bm{\varepsilon}_{3}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(\varsigma_{1}\hat{x}+\varsigma_{2}\hat{y}+\varsigma_{3}\hat{z}),\quad\bm{\varepsilon}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\varsigma_{1}\hat{x}-\varsigma_{2}\hat{y}),\quad\bm{\varepsilon}_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(\varsigma_{1}\hat{x}+\varsigma_{2}\hat{y}-2\varsigma_{3}\hat{z}),

(206)

any momentum will be expanded in these coordinates: denote
\bm{k}=k_{1}\hat{x}+k_{2}\hat{y}+k_{3}\hat{z}=c\bm{\varepsilon}_{3}+a(\bm{\varepsilon}_{1}\cos\theta+\bm{\varepsilon}_{2}\sin\theta)+b(-\bm{\varepsilon}_{1}\sin\theta+\bm{\varepsilon}_{2}\cos\theta)
, then we have

\displaystyle k_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varsigma_{1}\left(\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}+\eta\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}+\frac{b}{\sqrt{6}}\right)\cos\theta+\eta\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{6}}-\frac{b}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\sin\theta\right),

(207a)

\displaystyle k_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varsigma_{2}\left(\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}+\eta\left(-\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}+\frac{b}{\sqrt{6}}\right)\cos\theta+\eta\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{6}}+\frac{b}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\sin\theta\right),

(207b)

\displaystyle k_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varsigma_{3}\left(\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}-\eta\frac{2b}{\sqrt{6}}\cos\theta-\eta\frac{2a}{\sqrt{6}}\sin\theta\right).

(207c)

First, expand the Hamiltonian (
45a
) to first order of
\eta
and then set
\eta=1
, we obtain

\mathcal{H}=\sin\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}\left(\frac{(-a+\sqrt{3}b)\cos\theta+(\sqrt{3}a+b)\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma^{1}-\frac{(a+\sqrt{3}b)\cos\theta+(\sqrt{3}a-b)\sin\theta}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma^{2}+\sqrt{2}(a\cos\theta-b\sin\theta)\sigma^{3}\right)

(208)

with the energy
E^{2}(\bm{k})=3\sin^{2}\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}(a^{2}+b^{2})=v^{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})
where we defined
v\equiv\sqrt{3}\sin\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}
. From now on, for any
\bm{k}
dependent function
f=f(\bm{k})
, we will introduce the notation
f_{\pm}\equiv f(\bm{k}\pm\bm{q}/2)
.

Using the Feynman parameterization we have

\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)=\int\frac{d^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int^{1}_{0}du\frac{Z^{\mu\nu}(k,q)}{\left[u(k_{0+}^{2}+v_{+}^{2}(a_{+}^{2}+b_{+}^{2}))+(1-u)(k_{0-}^{2}+v_{-}^{2}(a_{-}^{2}+b_{-}^{2}))\right]^{2}},

(209)

where we defined

Z^{\mu\nu}=\mathrm{Tr}[\Gamma^{\mu}(\bm{k})(i(k_{0}+q_{0}/2)+H(\bm{k}+\bm{q}/2))\Gamma^{\nu}(\bm{k})(i(k_{0}-q_{0}/2)+H(\bm{k}-\bm{q}/2))].

(210)

In local coordinates,
\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)
then can be written as

\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)=\frac{\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{2}\varsigma_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int d\kappa_{0}\int\frac{d^{2}\bm{\kappa}_{\perp}}{g}\int d\kappa_{\parallel}\int^{1}_{0}du\frac{Z^{\mu\nu}(k,q)}{\left(\kappa^{2}_{0}+\bm{\kappa}^{2}_{\perp}+\Delta\right)^{2}},

(211)

where we defined

\kappa_{0}=k_{0}+(u-1/2)q_{0},\quad\bm{\kappa}_{\perp}=\sqrt{g}\left(\bm{k}_{\perp}+\frac{1}{2}f\bm{q}_{\perp}\right),\quad\kappa_{\parallel}=\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}},

(212)

with

g=uv_{+}^{2}+(1-u)v_{-}^{2},\quad h=uv_{+}^{2}-(1-u)v_{-}^{2},\quad f=\frac{h}{g}=\frac{uv_{+}^{2}-(1-u)v_{-}^{2}}{uv_{+}^{2}+(1-u)v_{-}^{2}},\quad\Delta=u(1-u)q^{2}_{0}+\frac{1}{4}(1-f^{2})gq^{2}_{\perp}.

(213)

The denominator has spherical symmetry with respect to
(\kappa_{0},\bm{\kappa}_{\perp})
. If we set
v_{+}=v_{-}\equiv v
then the isotropic (i.e. relativistic) limit is recovered:
\bm{\kappa}_{\perp}=\bm{k}_{\perp}+(u-1/2)\bm{q}_{\perp}
which agrees with
\kappa_{0}=k_{0}+(u-1/2)q_{0}
; and
\frac{1}{4}g(1-f^{2})q^{2}_{\perp}\rightarrow u(1-u)q^{2}_{\perp}
which agrees with
u(1-u)q_{0}^{2}
.

Now we simplify the numerator
Z^{\mu\nu}
. The vertex
\Gamma^{\mu}
in principle needs expansion according to powers of
\eta
, however for our purpose it suffices to keep the zeroth order, i.e.
\Gamma^{1}(\bm{k})=\sin k_{1}(\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{3})\sim\varsigma_{1}\sin\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}(\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{3})
, and similarly
\Gamma^{2}(\bm{k})\sim\varsigma_{2}\sin\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}(\sigma^{3}-\sigma^{1})
, and
\Gamma^{3}(\bm{k})\sim\varsigma_{3}\sin\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}(\sigma^{1}-\sigma^{2})
. For later convenience we further define
\varsigma_{0}\equiv 1
. This way in
Z^{\mu\nu}
there will be prefactors of

\varsigma_{\mu}\varsigma_{\nu}\left(\sin\frac{c}{\sqrt{3}}\right)^{\delta_{\mu\neq 0}+\delta_{\nu\neq 0}}=\varsigma_{\mu}\varsigma_{\nu}\left(\frac{v}{\sqrt{3}}\right)^{\delta_{\mu\neq 0}+\delta_{\nu\neq 0}}.

We then apply Eq. (
212
) and
Z^{\mu\nu}
will be written as polynomials of
\kappa
, up to quadratic order. Then only the constant terms in
\kappa
and the squared terms in
\kappa
needs to be kept since the other terms integrate to zero. The integral over
d^{3}\kappa=d\kappa_{0}d^{2}\bm{\kappa}_{\perp}
can then be evaluated. Using dimensional regularization

\int d^{3}\kappa\frac{\{\kappa^{2},1\}}{(\kappa^{2}+\Delta)^{2}}=4\pi\int d\kappa\frac{\{\kappa^{4},\kappa^{2}\}}{(\kappa^{2}+\Delta)^{2}}=\pi^{2}\{-3\Delta^{1/2},\frac{1}{\Delta^{1/2}}\}

(214)

where the divergent part of the first term has been subtracted (this part is independent of the external momentum
\bm{q}
and will cancel the divergence from the “tadpole” diagram). We then have

\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)=\frac{\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{2}\varsigma_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int d\kappa_{\parallel}\varsigma_{\mu}\varsigma_{\nu}\left(\frac{v}{\sqrt{3}}\right)^{\delta_{\mu\neq 0}+\delta_{\nu\neq 0}}\int^{1}_{0}duI^{\mu\nu}(\kappa_{\parallel},q),

(215)

where (note we have put back in
I^{\mu\nu}
the extra
\frac{1}{g}
in Eq. (
211
) resulted from the change of integral variables)

\displaystyle I^{00}

\displaystyle=4\pi^{2}\left(-I_{1}q_{0}^{2}+I_{2}q_{0}^{2}v_{-}v_{+}-\frac{1}{6}I_{2}Q^{2}v_{-}^{2}v_{+}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}I_{3}Q^{2}v_{-}^{3}v_{+}^{3}\right),

(216a)

\displaystyle I^{0i}

\displaystyle=-4\pi^{2}q_{0}Q_{i}v_{-}v_{+}\frac{v_{-}+v_{+}}{2\sqrt{3}}I_{2},\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad i\in\{1,2,3\},

(216b)

\displaystyle I^{ii}

\displaystyle=4\pi^{2}\left(2I_{1}q_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{3}I_{2}Q^{2}v_{-}^{2}v_{+}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}I_{3}Q_{ii}^{2}v_{-}^{3}v_{+}^{3}\right),\qquad\qquad\quad i\in\{1,2,3\},

(216c)

\displaystyle I^{ij}

\displaystyle=-4\pi^{2}\left(I_{1}q_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}I_{2}Q^{2}v_{-}^{2}v_{+}^{2}+\frac{1}{3}I_{3}Q_{kk}^{2}v_{-}^{3}v_{+}^{3}\right),\;\;\quad\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\},

(216d)

where the definitions of
Q
,
Q_{i}
and
Q_{ii}
are in Eqs. (
61
), and we defined

I_{1}=\frac{(1-u)u}{\sqrt{\Delta}g},\qquad I_{2}=\frac{(1-u)u}{\sqrt{\Delta}g^{2}},\qquad I_{3}=\frac{(1-u)u}{\sqrt{\Delta}g^{3}}.

(217)

We note that the integrals
\int^{1}_{0}I_{1,2,3}du
for general
v_{+}\neq v_{-}
can be evaluated, and the result is written in terms of the Elliptic functions. However we are allowed to set
v_{+}=v_{-}=v
in
I_{1,2,3}
since the
\bm{q}
dependence in
v_{\pm}
does not affect the leading order of the photon self energy
\Pi_{1}
which is linear in
\bm{q}
. Then we have

\int^{1}_{0}I_{1}du=\frac{\pi}{8v^{2}\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}},\qquad\int^{1}_{0}I_{2}du=\frac{\pi}{8v^{4}\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}},\qquad\int^{1}_{0}I_{3}du=\frac{\pi}{8v^{6}\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}},

(218)

This allows us to write

\int^{1}_{0}I^{\mu\nu}du=\frac{\pi^{3}}{2\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\frac{1}{3}Q^{2}&-\frac{q_{0}Q_{1}}{\sqrt{3}v}&-\frac{q_{0}Q_{2}}{\sqrt{3}v}&-\frac{q_{0}Q_{3}}{\sqrt{3}v}\\
-\frac{q_{0}Q_{1}}{\sqrt{3}v}&2\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{1}{3}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{11}^{2}&-\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}-\frac{1}{6}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{33}^{2}&-\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}-\frac{1}{6}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{22}^{2}\\
-\frac{q_{0}Q_{2}}{\sqrt{3}v}&-\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}-\frac{1}{6}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{33}^{2}&2\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{1}{3}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{22}^{2}&-\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}-\frac{1}{6}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{11}^{2}\\
-\frac{q_{0}Q_{3}}{\sqrt{3}v}&-\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}-\frac{1}{6}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{22}^{2}&-\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}-\frac{1}{6}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{11}^{2}&2\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{v^{2}}+\frac{1}{3}Q^{2}-\frac{1}{3}Q_{33}^{2}\end{array}\right).

(219)

The matrix on the right has two zero eigenvalues and two nonzero eigenvalues,
\frac{3\pi^{3}}{2v^{2}}\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}
and
\frac{3\pi^{3}}{2v^{2}}\frac{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{9}Q^{2}v^{2}}{\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}}
. And we have

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mu\nu}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{2}\varsigma_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int d\kappa_{\parallel}\frac{\pi^{3}}{2\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}}\times

(220)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\frac{1}{3}Q^{2}&-\varsigma_{1}\frac{q_{0}Q_{1}}{3}&-\varsigma_{2}\frac{q_{0}Q_{2}}{3}&-\varsigma_{3}\frac{q_{0}Q_{3}}{3}\\
-\varsigma_{1}\frac{q_{0}Q_{1}}{3}&\frac{2}{3}q_{0}^{2}+\frac{v^{2}}{9}(Q^{2}-Q_{11}^{2})&-\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{2}\left[\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{3}+\frac{v^{2}}{18}(Q^{2}+2Q_{33}^{2})\right]&-\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{3}\left[\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{3}-\frac{v^{2}}{18}(Q^{2}+2Q_{22}^{2})\right]\\
-\varsigma_{2}\frac{q_{0}Q_{2}}{3}&-\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{2}\left[\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{3}+\frac{v^{2}}{18}(Q^{2}+2Q_{33}^{2})\right]&\frac{2}{3}q_{0}^{2}+\frac{v^{2}}{9}(Q^{2}-Q_{22}^{2})&-\varsigma_{2}\varsigma_{3}\left[\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{3}+\frac{v^{2}}{18}(Q^{2}+2Q_{11}^{2})\right]\\
-\varsigma_{3}\frac{q_{0}Q_{3}}{3}&-\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{3}\left[\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{3}+\frac{v^{2}}{18}(Q^{2}+2Q_{22}^{2})\right]&-\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{2}\left[\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{3}+\frac{v^{2}}{18}(Q^{2}+2Q_{11}^{2})\right]&\frac{2}{3}q_{0}^{2}+\frac{v^{2}}{9}(Q^{2}-Q_{33}^{2})\end{array}\right).

The matrix now has two zero eigenvalues and two nonzero eigenvalues
\frac{\pi^{3}(q_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{3}Q^{2})}{2\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}}
and
\frac{\pi^{3}}{2}\sqrt{q_{0}^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{3}v^{2}}
.

The final integral is over
\kappa_{\parallel}
. To do this, cutoff must be imposed in the vicinity of
\Gamma
and
\mathrm{L}
points where the Dirac velocity vanishes:

\displaystyle\int^{\pi-\theta_{0}}_{\theta_{0}}d\kappa_{\parallel}\frac{1}{\sqrt{A+\sin^{2}\kappa_{\parallel}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{F(\pi-\theta_{0},-\frac{1}{A})-F(\theta_{0},-\frac{1}{A})}{\sqrt{A}},

(221a)

\displaystyle\int^{\pi-\theta_{0}}_{\theta_{0}}d\kappa_{\parallel}\frac{\sin^{2}\kappa_{\parallel}}{\sqrt{A+\sin^{2}\kappa_{\parallel}}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{A}\left(E(\pi-\theta_{0},-\frac{1}{A})-E(\theta_{0},-\frac{1}{A})-F(\pi-\theta_{0},-\frac{1}{A})+F(\theta_{0},-\frac{1}{A})\right),

(221b)

with
A=\frac{q^{2}_{0}}{Q^{2}}
. Note that we can safely set
\theta_{0}=0
in the second integral.
